People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um… well, the thing that finally scared me enough to actually come in is I’ve been hearing my name. Like, someone calling me from the hallway at home. And it’s not like a little thought in my head or something I’m imagining half-asleep—it sounds real, like as real as my husband standing right next to me saying it.

It’s happened at home, and then once it happened at the warehouse too, when I was working, and I looked around because I really thought somebody had called me. But nobody was there. That’s what got to me. I can deal with a lot of stress and weird days, you know, but hearing my name clear like that when there’s no one around… it really shook me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… not really anything else. It’s mainly just my name. And that’s part of why it’s so unsettling, because it’s not like a whole sentence or a conversation where I could maybe tell myself, okay, I’m just thinking too much or I heard the TV or something. It’s just clear—my name, like someone is right there calling me.

At home it sounds like it’s coming from the hallway, and I’ll actually stop what I’m doing and look, because it feels that real. And then when it happened at the warehouse too, that really bothered me, because I was awake, working, not lying in bed or half-asleep or anything. I thought someone needed me, but nobody was there. So no, not other voices or anything I can make out—just my name being called, and it sounds real enough that I react before I can even think about it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if it sounds disjointed to other people all the time, but inside my head it feels like everything is jumping tracks too fast.

Like I’ll be thinking about something from work—warehouse orders, what needs stocking, numbers, where things go—and then suddenly I’m thinking about yarn colors for something I was going to make, and then bills I need to pay, and then some old family names or people from years ago, and I don’t even know how I got there. It’s like my mind skips from one thing to the next before I can finish the first thought.

And then I’ll be standing there realizing I lost track of what I was doing. Like, I started out trying to do one simple thing, and somehow my brain has run through ten different subjects, and I can’t grab onto the original one anymore. It’s frustrating, because I’ll try to explain it and I can hear myself going in circles.

I can deal with a lot of stress and weird days, you know, but hearing my name clear like that when there’s no one around… it really shook me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear anything else besides your name being called?\n\nPatient: No… not really anything else. It’s mainly just my name. And that’s part of why it’s so unsettling, because it’s not like a whole sentence or a conversation where I could maybe tell myself, okay, I’m just thinking too much or I heard the TV or something. It’s just clear—my name, like someone is right there calling me.\n\nAt home it sounds like it’s coming from the hallway, and I’ll actually stop what I’m doing and look, because it feels that real. And then when it happened at the warehouse too, that really bothered me, because I was awake, working, not lying in bed or half-asleep or anything. I thought someone needed me, but nobody was there. So no, not other voices or anything I can make out—just my name being called, and it sounds real enough that I react before I can even think about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you seen anything unusual that wasn't really there?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t seen anything, not like… shapes or people or things moving that aren’t there. Like I said, hearing my name called from the hallway at home, and that one time at the warehouse when nobody was there. It sounds so real—like my husband is standing right next to me saying it—that I actually turn and look before I even think. But seeing things, no, I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually anxious, depressed, or irritable lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… depressed, I guess. It kind of comes after I’ve been running around nonstop, arguing, getting all worked up, and then it’s like I just crash. I don’t even know how else to describe it except heavy and empty at the same time.\n\nI’ll sit at the kitchen table and just cry over nothing—like literally nothing, no one even said anything to me right then—and I can’t seem to pull myself back together. It feels like I’m stuck there, like I don’t know how to get back to myself.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with the heaviness and emptiness?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say about three months that this depressed, withdrawn feeling has been hanging over me. Like not every single minute is the exact same, but it’s been there in the background, like this heaviness I can’t really shake off.\n\nBut these past few weeks are when it got a lot harder to manage. Before, I could sort of push through or tell myself I was just tired, keep doing what I had to do. Lately it feels like it takes over more, like I’m just sitting there feeling empty and heavy and I don’t have the energy to pull myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to express your thoughts or feel like your thoughts are disjointed or don't make sense to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Yeah… I think so. There have been times where I felt really fired up, like I had this big burst of energy and purpose, especially about my craft business. I’d get it in my head that I was finally going to make it work, like really make it work, and I’d start talking about all these plans and what needed to be done around the house so I could focus.

And it wasn’t just happy exactly—it was like I felt unstoppable, like nobody understood how important it was or how clear it all seemed to me. Then if my husband made even a small comment, like asking if I was sure about something or suggesting I slow down, I’d snap at him. I’d take it like he was trying to hold me back or get in my way, even if maybe he didn’t mean it that way.

So yeah, it was energetic, but also really irritable. Like everything felt urgent, and I didn’t want anyone questioning me.

Yes… yes, it did last several days. It wasn’t just like a good mood for an afternoon or something. At first I felt really fired up, like I had all this energy and I was finally seeing everything clearly, especially with my craft business. I felt almost unstoppable, like, “This is what I’m supposed to be doing, and everyone just needs to get on board.”

And it spilled into things at home too. I’d start making these rules or plans about how the house needed to run so I could focus, and in my mind it all made perfect sense. But if my husband made even the smallest comment—like questioning something or telling me maybe I should slow down—I’d snap at him. It felt like he was trying to hold me back, even if now I can kind of see maybe he was just asking a normal question. At the time, though, it felt personal, like he was getting in my way.

I mean, I don’t know if it sounds disjointed to other people all the time, but inside my head it feels like everything is jumping tracks too fast.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about something from work—warehouse orders, what needs stocking, numbers, where things go—and then suddenly I’m thinking about yarn colors for something I was going to make, and then bills I need to pay, and then some old family names or people from years ago, and I don’t even know how I got there. It’s like my mind skips from one thing to the next before I can finish the first thought.\n\nAnd then I’ll be standing there realizing I lost track of what I was doing. Like, I started out trying to do one simple thing, and somehow my brain has run through ten different subjects, and I can’t grab onto the original one anymore. It’s frustrating, because I’ll try to explain it and I can hear myself going in circles.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy, energetic, or irritable and didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’d get it in my head that I was finally going to make it work, like really make it work, and I’d start talking about all these plans and what needed to be done around the house so I could focus.\n\nAnd it wasn’t just happy exactly—it was like I felt unstoppable, like nobody understood how important it was or how clear it all seemed to me. Then if my husband made even a small comment, like asking if I was sure about something or suggesting I slow down, I’d snap at him. I’d take it like he was trying to hold me back or get in my way, even if maybe he didn’t mean it that way.\n\nSo yeah, it was energetic, but also really irritable. Like everything felt urgent, and I didn’t want anyone questioning me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy or energetic that lasted for several days?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it did last several days. It’s hard for me to put an exact number on it, because when I’m in it, it doesn’t feel like, “Oh, this is one of those periods,” you know? It feels like I’m just finally motivated and everyone else is being too slow or getting in the way.

But it has happened more than once. I’ll get these stretches where I’m really fired up, especially about my craft business, like I suddenly have all these plans and I’m almost unstoppable. And then I start bringing the house into it too, making rules or deciding how things need to be done so I can focus. At first it feels exciting, like I’ve got purpose.

But then it turns sharp pretty fast. My husband can make one small comment—just asking a question or saying maybe I should think it through—and I’ll snap at him like he’s trying to hold me back. So… not constantly, but it comes in periods, and when it does, it’s noticeable.

And then when it happened at the warehouse too, that really bothered me, because I was awake, working, not lying in bed or half-asleep or anything. I thought someone needed me, but nobody was there. So no, not other voices or anything I can make out—just my name being called, and it sounds real enough that I react before I can even think about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you seen anything unusual that wasn't really there?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t seen anything, not like… shapes or people or things moving that aren’t there. At least not that I can think of.\n\nIt’s more the hearing that’s been scaring me. Like I said, hearing my name called from the hallway at home, and that one time at the warehouse when nobody was there. It sounds so real—like my husband is standing right next to me saying it—that I actually turn and look before I even think. But seeing things, no, I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually anxious, depressed, or irritable lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… depressed, I guess. It kind of comes after I’ve been running around nonstop, arguing, getting all worked up, and then it’s like I just crash. I don’t even know how else to describe it except heavy and empty at the same time.\n\nI’ll sit at the kitchen table and just cry over nothing—like literally nothing, no one even said anything to me right then—and I can’t seem to pull myself back together. It feels like I’m stuck there, like I don’t know how to get back to myself.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with the heaviness and emptiness?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say about three months that this depressed, withdrawn feeling has been hanging over me. Like not every single minute is the exact same, but it’s been there in the background, like this heaviness I can’t really shake off.\n\nBut these past few weeks are when it got a lot harder to manage. Before, I could sort of push through or tell myself I was just tired, keep doing what I had to do. Lately it feels like it takes over more, like I’m just sitting there feeling empty and heavy and I don’t have the energy to pull myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to express your thoughts or feel like your thoughts are disjointed or don't make sense to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if it sounds disjointed to other people all the time, but inside my head it feels like everything is jumping tracks too fast.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about something from work—warehouse orders, what needs stocking, numbers, where things go—and then suddenly I’m thinking about yarn colors for something I was going to make, and then bills I need to pay, and then some old family names or people from years ago, and I don’t even know how I got there. It’s like my mind skips from one thing to the next before I can finish the first thought.\n\nAnd then I’ll be standing there realizing I lost track of what I was doing. Like, I started out trying to do one simple thing, and somehow my brain has run through ten different subjects, and I can’t grab onto the original one anymore. It’s frustrating, because I’ll try to explain it and I can hear myself going in circles.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy, energetic, or irritable and didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. There have been times where I felt really fired up, like I had this big burst of energy and purpose, especially about my craft business. I’d get it in my head that I was finally going to make it work, like really make it work, and I’d start talking about all these plans and what needed to be done around the house so I could focus.\n\nAnd it wasn’t just happy exactly—it was like I felt unstoppable, like nobody understood how important it was or how clear it all seemed to me. Then if my husband made even a small comment, like asking if I was sure about something or suggesting I slow down, I’d snap at him. I’d take it like he was trying to hold me back or get in my way, even if maybe he didn’t mean it that way.\n\nSo yeah, it was energetic, but also really irritable. Like everything felt urgent, and I didn’t want anyone questioning me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy or energetic that lasted for several days?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it did last several days. It wasn’t just like a good mood for an afternoon or something. At first I felt really fired up, like I had all this energy and I was finally seeing everything clearly, especially with my craft business. I felt almost unstoppable, like, “This is what I’m supposed to be doing, and everyone just needs to get on board.”\n\nAnd it spilled into things at home too. I’d start making these rules or plans about how the house needed to run so I could focus, and in my mind it all made perfect sense. But if my husband made even the smallest comment—like questioning something or telling me maybe I should slow down—I’d snap at him. It felt like he was trying to hold me back, even if now I can kind of see maybe he was just asking a normal question. At the time, though, it felt personal, like he was getting in my way.\n\nDoctor: How often do these periods of feeling extremely happy or energetic happen?\n\nPatient: It’s hard for me to put an exact number on it, because when I’m in it, it doesn’t feel like, “Oh, this is one of those periods,” you know? It feels like I’m just finally motivated and everyone else is being too slow or getting in the way.\n\nBut it has happened more than once. I’ll get these stretches where I’m really fired up, especially about my craft business, like I suddenly have all these plans and I’m almost unstoppable. And then I start bringing the house into it too, making rules or deciding how things need to be done so I can focus. At first it feels exciting, like I’ve got purpose.\n\nBut then it turns sharp pretty fast. My husband can make one small comment—just asking a question or saying maybe I should think it through—and I’ll snap at him like he’s trying to hold me back. So… not constantly, but it comes in periods, and when it does, it’s noticeable.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… usually I do. It’s like after I’ve been running around with all that energy, making plans, arguing, pushing everybody, then suddenly there’s nothing there holding me up anymore.

I’ll end up just sitting at the kitchen table, and it feels heavy… like this empty feeling comes over me. I’ll cry over things that don’t even make sense, or sometimes over nothing I can explain. It’s embarrassing because a few days before I might’ve been so sure of myself and so intense, and then afterward I can’t seem to get myself back. It feels like I used everything up, and then I’m just… flat and sad and stuck there.

It feels like I’m stuck there, like I don’t know how to get back to myself.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with the heaviness and emptiness?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say about three months that this depressed, withdrawn feeling has been hanging over me. Like not every single minute is the exact same, but it’s been there in the background, like this heaviness I can’t really shake off.\n\nBut these past few weeks are when it got a lot harder to manage. Before, I could sort of push through or tell myself I was just tired, keep doing what I had to do. Lately it feels like it takes over more, like I’m just sitting there feeling empty and heavy and I don’t have the energy to pull myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to express your thoughts or feel like your thoughts are disjointed or don't make sense to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if it sounds disjointed to other people all the time, but inside my head it feels like everything is jumping tracks too fast.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about something from work—warehouse orders, what needs stocking, numbers, where things go—and then suddenly I’m thinking about yarn colors for something I was going to make, and then bills I need to pay, and then some old family names or people from years ago, and I don’t even know how I got there. It’s like my mind skips from one thing to the next before I can finish the first thought.\n\nAnd then I’ll be standing there realizing I lost track of what I was doing. Like, I started out trying to do one simple thing, and somehow my brain has run through ten different subjects, and I can’t grab onto the original one anymore. It’s frustrating, because I’ll try to explain it and I can hear myself going in circles.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy, energetic, or irritable and didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure if I can say exactly “four times,” like I haven’t been keeping a calendar of it. But it does happen in these stretches where I get really fired up, especially about my craft business, and then it kind of takes over everything.

At first it feels good, like I’m finally on track and I can handle all of it, the business and the house and everybody just needs to follow the plan. I’ll start making rules about how things should be done at home so I can focus, and in my head it feels completely reasonable. But then if my husband says even one small thing—like questioning a plan or saying maybe I’m doing too much—I’ll snap at him. I really feel in that moment like he’s trying to hold me back.

So… I don’t know if I can confidently count it as at least four times a year, but it’s happened enough that it’s a pattern, not just a one-time thing.

It kind of comes after I’ve been running around nonstop, arguing, getting all worked up, and then it’s like I just crash. I don’t even know how else to describe it except heavy and empty at the same time.\n\nI’ll sit at the kitchen table and just cry over nothing—like literally nothing, no one even said anything to me right then—and I can’t seem to pull myself back together. It feels like I’m stuck there, like I don’t know how to get back to myself.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with the heaviness and emptiness?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say about three months that this depressed, withdrawn feeling has been hanging over me. Lately it feels like it takes over more, like I’m just sitting there feeling empty and heavy and I don’t have the energy to pull myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to express your thoughts or feel like your thoughts are disjointed or don't make sense to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if it sounds disjointed to other people all the time, but inside my head it feels like everything is jumping tracks too fast.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about something from work—warehouse orders, what needs stocking, numbers, where things go—and then suddenly I’m thinking about yarn colors for something I was going to make, and then bills I need to pay, and then some old family names or people from years ago, and I don’t even know how I got there. It’s like my mind skips from one thing to the next before I can finish the first thought.\n\nAnd then I’ll be standing there realizing I lost track of what I was doing. I mean, I don’t know if it sounds disjointed to other people all the time, but inside my head it feels like everything is jumping tracks too fast.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about something from work—warehouse orders, what needs stocking, numbers, where things go—and then suddenly I’m thinking about yarn colors for something I was going to make, and then bills I need to pay, and then some old family names or people from years ago, and I don’t even know how I got there. It’s like my mind skips from one thing to the next before I can finish the first thought.\n\nAnd then I’ll be standing there realizing I lost track of what I was doing. Like, I started out trying to do one simple thing, and somehow my brain has run through ten different subjects, and I can’t grab onto the original one anymore. It’s frustrating, because I’ll try to explain it and I can hear myself going in circles.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy, energetic, or irritable and didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. There have been times where I felt really fired up, like I had this big burst of energy and purpose, especially about my craft business. I’d get it in my head that I was finally going to make it work, like really make it work, and I’d start talking about all these plans and what needed to be done around the house so I could focus.\n\nAnd it wasn’t just happy exactly—it was like I felt unstoppable, like nobody understood how important it was or how clear it all seemed to me. Then if my husband made even a small comment, like asking if I was sure about something or suggesting I slow down, I’d snap at him. I’d take it like he was trying to hold me back or get in my way, even if maybe he didn’t mean it that way.\n\nSo yeah, it was energetic, but also really irritable. Like everything felt urgent, and I didn’t want anyone questioning me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy or energetic that lasted for several days?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it did last several days. It wasn’t just like a good mood for an afternoon or something. At first I felt really fired up, like I had all this energy and I was finally seeing everything clearly, especially with my craft business. I felt almost unstoppable, like, “This is what I’m supposed to be doing, and everyone just needs to get on board.”\n\nAnd it spilled into things at home too. I’d start making these rules or plans about how the house needed to run so I could focus, and in my mind it all made perfect sense. But if my husband made even the smallest comment—like questioning something or telling me maybe I should slow down—I’d snap at him. It felt like he was trying to hold me back, even if now I can kind of see maybe he was just asking a normal question. At the time, though, it felt personal, like he was getting in my way.\n\nDoctor: How often do these periods of feeling extremely happy or energetic happen?\n\nPatient: It’s hard for me to put an exact number on it, because when I’m in it, it doesn’t feel like, “Oh, this is one of those periods,” you know? It feels like I’m just finally motivated and everyone else is being too slow or getting in the way.\n\nBut it has happened more than once. I’ll get these stretches where I’m really fired up, especially about my craft business, like I suddenly have all these plans and I’m almost unstoppable. And then I start bringing the house into it too, making rules or deciding how things need to be done so I can focus. At first it feels exciting, like I’ve got purpose.\n\nBut then it turns sharp pretty fast. My husband can make one small comment—just asking a question or saying maybe I should think it through—and I’ll snap at him like he’s trying to hold me back. So… not constantly, but it comes in periods, and when it does, it’s noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel a downturn or a crash after these periods of high energy and motivation?\n\nPatient: Yes… usually I do. It’s like after I’ve been running around with all that energy, making plans, arguing, pushing everybody, then suddenly there’s nothing there holding me up anymore.\n\nI’ll end up just sitting at the kitchen table, and it feels heavy… like this empty feeling comes over me. I’ll cry over things that don’t even make sense, or sometimes over nothing I can explain. It’s embarrassing because a few days before I might’ve been so sure of myself and so intense, and then afterward I can’t seem to get myself back. It feels like I used everything up, and then I’m just… flat and sad and stuck there.\n\nDoctor: Do these periods of feeling extremely happy or energetic happen at least four times a year?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure if I can say exactly “four times,” like I haven’t been keeping a calendar of it. But it does happen in these stretches where I get really fired up, especially about my craft business, and then it kind of takes over everything.\n\nAt first it feels good, like I’m finally on track and I can handle all of it, the business and the house and everybody just needs to follow the plan. I’ll start making rules about how things should be done at home so I can focus, and in my head it feels completely reasonable. But then if my husband says even one small thing—like questioning a plan or saying maybe I’m doing too much—I’ll snap at him. I really feel in that moment like he’s trying to hold me back.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I can confidently count it as at least four times a year, but it’s happened enough that it’s a pattern, not just a one-time thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel depressed after these periods of high energy are over?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. That’s actually one of the parts that scares me a little, because when the energy is there, I don’t really see it as a problem in the moment. I’m just moving, planning, pushing things forward, arguing if I feel like people are slowing me down. But after it’s over, it’s like everything drops out from under me.\n\nI’ll be sitting at the kitchen table, and it’s just this heavy, empty feeling. I think… yes, or at least they come pretty tied together for me. It feels like I’m just finally motivated and everyone else is being too slow or getting in the way.\n\nBut it has happened more than once. I’ll get these stretches where I’m really fired up, especially about my craft business, like I suddenly have all these plans and I’m almost unstoppable. And then I start bringing the house into it too, making rules or deciding how things need to be done so I can focus. At first it feels exciting, like I’ve got purpose.\n\nBut then it turns sharp pretty fast. My husband can make one small comment—just asking a question or saying maybe I should think it through—and I’ll snap at him like he’s trying to hold me back. So… not constantly, but it comes in periods, and when it does, it’s noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel a downturn or a crash after these periods of high energy and motivation?\n\nPatient: Yes… usually I do. It’s like after I’ve been running around with all that energy, making plans, arguing, pushing everybody, then suddenly there’s nothing there holding me up anymore.\n\nI’ll end up just sitting at the kitchen table, and it feels heavy… like this empty feeling comes over me. I’ll cry over things that don’t even make sense, or sometimes over nothing I can explain. It’s embarrassing because a few days before I might’ve been so sure of myself and so intense, and then afterward I can’t seem to get myself back. It feels like I used everything up, and then I’m just… flat and sad and stuck there.\n\nDoctor: Do these periods of feeling extremely happy or energetic happen at least four times a year?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure if I can say exactly “four times,” like I haven’t been keeping a calendar of it. But it does happen in these stretches where I get really fired up, especially about my craft business, and then it kind of takes over everything.\n\nAt first it feels good, like I’m finally on track and I can handle all of it, the business and the house and everybody just needs to follow the plan. I’ll start making rules about how things should be done at home so I can focus, and in my head it feels completely reasonable. But then if my husband says even one small thing—like questioning a plan or saying maybe I’m doing too much—I’ll snap at him. I really feel in that moment like he’s trying to hold me back.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I can confidently count it as at least four times a year, but it’s happened enough that it’s a pattern, not just a one-time thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel depressed after these periods of high energy are over?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. That’s actually one of the parts that scares me a little, because when the energy is there, I don’t really see it as a problem in the moment. I’m just moving, planning, pushing things forward, arguing if I feel like people are slowing me down. But after it’s over, it’s like everything drops out from under me.\n\nI’ll be sitting at the kitchen table, and it’s just this heavy, empty feeling. Like I used everything up and there’s nothing left in me. And I’ll cry—sometimes I don’t even know what I’m crying about. It’s not always one specific thing. It’s more like I can’t get myself back to normal, and I feel stuck there, ashamed and exhausted and sad.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your periods of feeling depressed happen just as often as your periods of high energy?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, or at least they come pretty tied together for me. It’s hard for me to count them like one-for-one, because when I’m in that high-energy part, I’m not thinking, “Oh, this is an episode” or anything. I’m just going, going, going—running around, getting things done, arguing, pushing whatever plan I’ve got in my head.\n\nBut then afterward, almost like my body and mind just give out, I drop into that heavy, empty feeling. I’ll sit at the kitchen table and just feel like there’s nothing left in me. And I cry over nothing sometimes, or over things that don’t really explain how bad I feel. It’s like I can’t get myself back. So I wouldn’t say I’ve tracked it perfectly, but the depressed part does seem to follow those high-energy stretches pretty often.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s been about three weeks this time, I think. Not all exactly the same every single day, but the whole stretch of it — being wound up and restless, like I can’t settle myself — and then it getting stranger, like hearing my name sometimes, and then afterward just crashing… that’s been going on for around three weeks.

I don’t know if I’m explaining it right, because it’s not like I wake up and mark a start date, but looking back, it feels like about three weeks where I haven’t really felt like myself.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… well, the thing that finally scared me enough to actually come in is I’ve been hearing my name. Like, someone calling me from the hallway at home. And it’s not like a little thought in my head or something I’m imagining half-asleep—it sounds real, like as real as my husband standing right next to me saying it.\n\nIt’s happened at home, and then once it happened at the warehouse too, when I was working, and I looked around because I really thought somebody had called me. But nobody was there. That’s what got to me. I can deal with a lot of stress and weird days, you know, but hearing my name clear like that when there’s no one around… it really shook me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear anything else besides your name being called?\n\nPatient: No… not really anything else. It’s mainly just my name. And that’s part of why it’s so unsettling, because it’s not like a whole sentence or a conversation where I could maybe tell myself, okay, I’m just thinking too much or I heard the TV or something. It’s just clear—my name, like someone is right there calling me.\n\nAt home it sounds like it’s coming from the hallway, and I’ll actually stop what I’m doing and look, because it feels that real. And then when it happened at the warehouse too, that really bothered me, because I was awake, working, not lying in bed or half-asleep or anything. I thought someone needed me, but nobody was there. So no, not other voices or anything I can make out—just my name being called, and it sounds real enough that I react before I can even think about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you seen anything unusual that wasn't really there?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t seen anything, not like… shapes or people or things moving that aren’t there. At least not that I can think of.\n\nIt’s more the hearing that’s been scaring me. Like I said, hearing my name called from the hallway at home, and that one time at the warehouse when nobody was there. It sounds so real—like my husband is standing right next to me saying it—that I actually turn and look before I even think. But seeing things, no, I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually anxious, depressed, or irritable lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… depressed, I guess. It kind of comes after I’ve been running around nonstop, arguing, getting all worked up, and then it’s like I just crash. I don’t even know how else to describe it except heavy and empty at the same time.\n\nI’ll sit at the kitchen table and just cry over nothing—like literally nothing, no one even said anything to me right then—and I can’t seem to pull myself back together. It feels like I’m stuck there, like I don’t know how to get back to myself.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with the heaviness and emptiness?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say about three months that this depressed, withdrawn feeling has been hanging over me. Like not every single minute is the exact same, but it’s been there in the background, like this heaviness I can’t really shake off.\n\nBut these past few weeks are when it got a lot harder to manage. Before, I could sort of push through or tell myself I was just tired, keep doing what I had to do. Lately it feels like it takes over more, like I’m just sitting there feeling empty and heavy and I don’t have the energy to pull myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to express your thoughts or feel like your thoughts are disjointed or don't make sense to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if it sounds disjointed to other people all the time, but inside my head it feels like everything is jumping tracks too fast.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about something from work—warehouse orders, what needs stocking, numbers, where things go—and then suddenly I’m thinking about yarn colors for something I was going to make, and then bills I need to pay, and then some old family names or people from years ago, and I don’t even know how I got there. It’s like my mind skips from one thing to the next before I can finish the first thought.\n\nAnd then I’ll be standing there realizing I lost track of what I was doing. Like, I started out trying to do one simple thing, and somehow my brain has run through ten different subjects, and I can’t grab onto the original one anymore. It’s frustrating, because I’ll try to explain it and I can hear myself going in circles.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy, energetic, or irritable and didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. There have been times where I felt really fired up, like I had this big burst of energy and purpose, especially about my craft business. I’d get it in my head that I was finally going to make it work, like really make it work, and I’d start talking about all these plans and what needed to be done around the house so I could focus.\n\nAnd it wasn’t just happy exactly—it was like I felt unstoppable, like nobody understood how important it was or how clear it all seemed to me. Then if my husband made even a small comment, like asking if I was sure about something or suggesting I slow down, I’d snap at him. I’d take it like he was trying to hold me back or get in my way, even if maybe he didn’t mean it that way.\n\nSo yeah, it was energetic, but also really irritable. Like everything felt urgent, and I didn’t want anyone questioning me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy or energetic that lasted for several days?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it did last several days. It wasn’t just like a good mood for an afternoon or something. At first I felt really fired up, like I had all this energy and I was finally seeing everything clearly, especially with my craft business. I felt almost unstoppable, like, “This is what I’m supposed to be doing, and everyone just needs to get on board.”\n\nAnd it spilled into things at home too. I’d start making these rules or plans about how the house needed to run so I could focus, and in my mind it all made perfect sense. But if my husband made even the smallest comment—like questioning something or telling me maybe I should slow down—I’d snap at him. It felt like he was trying to hold me back, even if now I can kind of see maybe he was just asking a normal question. At the time, though, it felt personal, like he was getting in my way.\n\nDoctor: How often do these periods of feeling extremely happy or energetic happen?\n\nPatient: It’s hard for me to put an exact number on it, because when I’m in it, it doesn’t feel like, “Oh, this is one of those periods,” you know? It feels like I’m just finally motivated and everyone else is being too slow or getting in the way.\n\nBut it has happened more than once. I’ll get these stretches where I’m really fired up, especially about my craft business, like I suddenly have all these plans and I’m almost unstoppable. And then I start bringing the house into it too, making rules or deciding how things need to be done so I can focus. At first it feels exciting, like I’ve got purpose.\n\nBut then it turns sharp pretty fast. My husband can make one small comment—just asking a question or saying maybe I should think it through—and I’ll snap at him like he’s trying to hold me back. So… not constantly, but it comes in periods, and when it does, it’s noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel a downturn or a crash after these periods of high energy and motivation?\n\nPatient: Yes… usually I do. It’s like after I’ve been running around with all that energy, making plans, arguing, pushing everybody, then suddenly there’s nothing there holding me up anymore.\n\nI’ll end up just sitting at the kitchen table, and it feels heavy… like this empty feeling comes over me. I’ll cry over things that don’t even make sense, or sometimes over nothing I can explain. It’s embarrassing because a few days before I might’ve been so sure of myself and so intense, and then afterward I can’t seem to get myself back. It feels like I used everything up, and then I’m just… flat and sad and stuck there.\n\nDoctor: Do these periods of feeling extremely happy or energetic happen at least four times a year?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure if I can say exactly “four times,” like I haven’t been keeping a calendar of it. But it does happen in these stretches where I get really fired up, especially about my craft business, and then it kind of takes over everything.\n\nAt first it feels good, like I’m finally on track and I can handle all of it, the business and the house and everybody just needs to follow the plan. I’ll start making rules about how things should be done at home so I can focus, and in my head it feels completely reasonable. But then if my husband says even one small thing—like questioning a plan or saying maybe I’m doing too much—I’ll snap at him. I really feel in that moment like he’s trying to hold me back.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I can confidently count it as at least four times a year, but it’s happened enough that it’s a pattern, not just a one-time thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel depressed after these periods of high energy are over?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. That’s actually one of the parts that scares me a little, because when the energy is there, I don’t really see it as a problem in the moment. I’m just moving, planning, pushing things forward, arguing if I feel like people are slowing me down. But after it’s over, it’s like everything drops out from under me.\n\nI’ll be sitting at the kitchen table, and it’s just this heavy, empty feeling. Like I used everything up and there’s nothing left in me. And I’ll cry—sometimes I don’t even know what I’m crying about. It’s not always one specific thing. It’s more like I can’t get myself back to normal, and I feel stuck there, ashamed and exhausted and sad.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your periods of feeling depressed happen just as often as your periods of high energy?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, or at least they come pretty tied together for me. It’s hard for me to count them like one-for-one, because when I’m in that high-energy part, I’m not thinking, “Oh, this is an episode” or anything. I’m just going, going, going—running around, getting things done, arguing, pushing whatever plan I’ve got in my head.\n\nBut then afterward, almost like my body and mind just give out, I drop into that heavy, empty feeling. It’s like I can’t get myself back. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really… or if I do, it doesn’t feel like it lasts long enough for me to trust it. After all that running around, pushing, arguing with people, feeling like I have to keep doing things, I don’t just land back in the middle. I kind of fall past normal, if that makes sense.

It’s just like everything in me got used up, and I can’t get myself back to how I’m supposed to be. So I guess between them, I’m not really “normal” so much as worn out and sad and trying to pull myself back together.

It’s like my mind skips from one thing to the next before I can finish the first thought.\n\nAnd then I’ll be standing there realizing I lost track of what I was doing. Then if my husband made even a small comment, like asking if I was sure about something or suggesting I slow down, I’d snap at him. I’d take it like he was trying to hold me back or get in my way, even if maybe he didn’t mean it that way.\n\nSo yeah, it was energetic, but also really irritable. Like everything felt urgent, and I didn’t want anyone questioning me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy or energetic that lasted for several days?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it did last several days. It wasn’t just like a good mood for an afternoon or something. At first I felt really fired up, like I had all this energy and I was finally seeing everything clearly, especially with my craft business. I felt almost unstoppable, like, “This is what I’m supposed to be doing, and everyone just needs to get on board.”\n\nAnd it spilled into things at home too. I’d start making these rules or plans about how the house needed to run so I could focus, and in my mind it all made perfect sense. But if my husband made even the smallest comment—like questioning something or telling me maybe I should slow down—I’d snap at him. It felt like he was trying to hold me back, even if now I can kind of see maybe he was just asking a normal question. At the time, though, it felt personal, like he was getting in my way.\n\nDoctor: How often do these periods of feeling extremely happy or energetic happen?\n\nPatient: It’s hard for me to put an exact number on it, because when I’m in it, it doesn’t feel like, “Oh, this is one of those periods,” you know? It feels like I’m just finally motivated and everyone else is being too slow or getting in the way.\n\nBut it has happened more than once. I’ll get these stretches where I’m really fired up, especially about my craft business, like I suddenly have all these plans and I’m almost unstoppable. And then I start bringing the house into it too, making rules or deciding how things need to be done so I can focus. At first it feels exciting, like I’ve got purpose.\n\nBut then it turns sharp pretty fast. I’ll cry over things that don’t even make sense, or sometimes over nothing I can explain. Before, I could sort of push through or tell myself I was just tired, keep doing what I had to do. Lately it feels like it takes over more, like I’m just sitting there feeling empty and heavy and I don’t have the energy to pull myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to express your thoughts or feel like your thoughts are disjointed or don't make sense to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if it sounds disjointed to other people all the time, but inside my head it feels like everything is jumping tracks too fast.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about something from work—warehouse orders, what needs stocking, numbers, where things go—and then suddenly I’m thinking about yarn colors for something I was going to make, and then bills I need to pay, and then some old family names or people from years ago, and I don’t even know how I got there. It’s like my mind skips from one thing to the next before I can finish the first thought.\n\nAnd then I’ll be standing there realizing I lost track of what I was doing. Like, I started out trying to do one simple thing, and somehow my brain has run through ten different subjects, and I can’t grab onto the original one anymore. It’s frustrating, because I’ll try to explain it and I can hear myself going in circles.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy, energetic, or irritable and didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. There have been times where I felt really fired up, like I had this big burst of energy and purpose, especially about my craft business. I’d get it in my head that I was finally going to make it work, like really make it work, and I’d start talking about all these plans and what needed to be done around the house so I could focus.\n\nAnd it wasn’t just happy exactly—it was like I felt unstoppable, like nobody understood how important it was or how clear it all seemed to me. Then if my husband made even a small comment, like asking if I was sure about something or suggesting I slow down, I’d snap at him. I’d take it like he was trying to hold me back or get in my way, even if maybe he didn’t mean it that way.\n\nSo yeah, it was energetic, but also really irritable. Like everything felt urgent, and I didn’t want anyone questioning me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy or energetic that lasted for several days?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it did last several days. It wasn’t just like a good mood for an afternoon or something. At first I felt really fired up, like I had all this energy and I was finally seeing everything clearly, especially with my craft business. I felt almost unstoppable, like, “This is what I’m supposed to be doing, and everyone just needs to get on board.”\n\nAnd it spilled into things at home too. I’d start making these rules or plans about how the house needed to run so I could focus, and in my mind it all made perfect sense. But if my husband made even the smallest comment—like questioning something or telling me maybe I should slow down—I’d snap at him. It felt like he was trying to hold me back, even if now I can kind of see maybe he was just asking a normal question. At the time, though, it felt personal, like he was getting in my way.\n\nDoctor: How often do these periods of feeling extremely happy or energetic happen?\n\nPatient: It’s hard for me to put an exact number on it, because when I’m in it, it doesn’t feel like, “Oh, this is one of those periods,” you know? It feels like I’m just finally motivated and everyone else is being too slow or getting in the way.\n\nBut it has happened more than once. I’ll get these stretches where I’m really fired up, especially about my craft business, like I suddenly have all these plans and I’m almost unstoppable. And then I start bringing the house into it too, making rules or deciding how things need to be done so I can focus. At first it feels exciting, like I’ve got purpose.\n\nBut then it turns sharp pretty fast. My husband can make one small comment—just asking a question or saying maybe I should think it through—and I’ll snap at him like he’s trying to hold me back. So… not constantly, but it comes in periods, and when it does, it’s noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel a downturn or a crash after these periods of high energy and motivation?\n\nPatient: Yes… usually I do. It’s like after I’ve been running around with all that energy, making plans, arguing, pushing everybody, then suddenly there’s nothing there holding me up anymore.\n\nI’ll end up just sitting at the kitchen table, and it feels heavy… like this empty feeling comes over me. I’ll cry over things that don’t even make sense, or sometimes over nothing I can explain. It’s embarrassing because a few days before I might’ve been so sure of myself and so intense, and then afterward I can’t seem to get myself back. It feels like I used everything up, and then I’m just… flat and sad and stuck there.\n\nDoctor: Do these periods of feeling extremely happy or energetic happen at least four times a year?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure if I can say exactly “four times,” like I haven’t been keeping a calendar of it. But it does happen in these stretches where I get really fired up, especially about my craft business, and then it kind of takes over everything.\n\nAt first it feels good, like I’m finally on track and I can handle all of it, the business and the house and everybody just needs to follow the plan. I’ll start making rules about how things should be done at home so I can focus, and in my head it feels completely reasonable. But then if my husband says even one small thing—like questioning a plan or saying maybe I’m doing too much—I’ll snap at him. I really feel in that moment like he’s trying to hold me back.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I can confidently count it as at least four times a year, but it’s happened enough that it’s a pattern, not just a one-time thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel depressed after these periods of high energy are over?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. That’s actually one of the parts that scares me a little, because when the energy is there, I don’t really see it as a problem in the moment. I’m just moving, planning, pushing things forward, arguing if I feel like people are slowing me down. But after it’s over, it’s like everything drops out from under me.\n\nI’ll be sitting at the kitchen table, and it’s just this heavy, empty feeling. Like I used everything up and there’s nothing left in me. And I’ll cry—sometimes I don’t even know what I’m crying about. It’s not always one specific thing. It’s more like I can’t get myself back to normal, and I feel stuck there, ashamed and exhausted and sad.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your periods of feeling depressed happen just as often as your periods of high energy?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, or at least they come pretty tied together for me. It’s hard for me to count them like one-for-one, because when I’m in that high-energy part, I’m not thinking, “Oh, this is an episode” or anything. I’m just going, going, going—running around, getting things done, arguing, pushing whatever plan I’ve got in my head.\n\nBut then afterward, almost like my body and mind just give out, I drop into that heavy, empty feeling. I’ll sit at the kitchen table and just feel like there’s nothing left in me. And I cry over nothing sometimes, or over things that don’t really explain how bad I feel. It’s like I can’t get myself back. So I wouldn’t say I’ve tracked it perfectly, but the depressed part does seem to follow those high-energy stretches pretty often.\n\nDoctor: How long do these periods of high energy typically last?\n\nPatient: It’s been about three weeks this time, I think. Not all exactly the same every single day, but the whole stretch of it — being wound up and restless, like I can’t settle myself — and then it getting stranger, like hearing my name sometimes, and then afterward just crashing… that’s been going on for around three weeks.\n\nI don’t know if I’m explaining it right, because it’s not like I wake up and mark a start date, but looking back, it feels like about three weeks where I haven’t really felt like myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you return to a normal state between these periods of high energy?\n\nPatient: Not really… or if I do, it doesn’t feel like it lasts long enough for me to trust it. After all that running around, pushing, arguing with people, feeling like I have to keep doing things, I don’t just land back in the middle. I kind of fall past normal, if that makes sense.\n\nI end up at the kitchen table a lot, just sitting there, and it’s like this heavy, empty feeling comes over me. I’ll cry, and sometimes there isn’t even a real reason I can point to. It’s just like everything in me got used up, and I can’t get myself back to how I’m supposed to be. So I guess between them, I’m not really “normal” so much as worn out and sad and trying to pull myself back together.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

It’s frustrating, because I’ll try to explain it and I can hear myself going in circles.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy, energetic, or irritable and didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. There have been times where I felt really fired up, like I had this big burst of energy and purpose, especially about my craft business. I’d get it in my head that I was finally going to make it work, like really make it work, and I’d start talking about all these plans and what needed to be done around the house so I could focus.\n\nAnd it wasn’t just happy exactly—it was like I felt unstoppable, like nobody understood how important it was or how clear it all seemed to me. Then if my husband made even a small comment, like asking if I was sure about something or suggesting I slow down, I’d snap at him. I’d take it like he was trying to hold me back or get in my way, even if maybe he didn’t mean it that way.\n\nSo yeah, it was energetic, but also really irritable. Like everything felt urgent, and I didn’t want anyone questioning me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy or energetic that lasted for several days?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it did last several days. It wasn’t just like a good mood for an afternoon or something. At first I felt really fired up, like I had all this energy and I was finally seeing everything clearly, especially with my craft business. I felt almost unstoppable, like, “This is what I’m supposed to be doing, and everyone just needs to get on board.”\n\nAnd it spilled into things at home too. I’d start making these rules or plans about how the house needed to run so I could focus, and in my mind it all made perfect sense. But if my husband made even the smallest comment—like questioning something or telling me maybe I should slow down—I’d snap at him. It felt like he was trying to hold me back, even if now I can kind of see maybe he was just asking a normal question. At the time, though, it felt personal, like he was getting in my way.\n\nDoctor: How often do these periods of feeling extremely happy or energetic happen?\n\nPatient: It’s hard for me to put an exact number on it, because when I’m in it, it doesn’t feel like, “Oh, this is one of those periods,” you know? It feels like I’m just finally motivated and everyone else is being too slow or getting in the way.\n\nBut it has happened more than once. I’ll get these stretches where I’m really fired up, especially about my craft business, like I suddenly have all these plans and I’m almost unstoppable. And then I start bringing the house into it too, making rules or deciding how things need to be done so I can focus. At first it feels exciting, like I’ve got purpose.\n\nBut then it turns sharp pretty fast. My husband can make one small comment—just asking a question or saying maybe I should think it through—and I’ll snap at him like he’s trying to hold me back. So… not constantly, but it comes in periods, and when it does, it’s noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel a downturn or a crash after these periods of high energy and motivation?\n\nPatient: Yes… usually I do. It’s like after I’ve been running around with all that energy, making plans, arguing, pushing everybody, then suddenly there’s nothing there holding me up anymore.\n\nI’ll end up just sitting at the kitchen table, and it feels heavy… like this empty feeling comes over me. I’ll cry over things that don’t even make sense, or sometimes over nothing I can explain. It’s embarrassing because a few days before I might’ve been so sure of myself and so intense, and then afterward I can’t seem to get myself back. It feels like I used everything up, and then I’m just… flat and sad and stuck there.\n\nDoctor: Do these periods of feeling extremely happy or energetic happen at least four times a year?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure if I can say exactly “four times,” like I haven’t been keeping a calendar of it. But it does happen in these stretches where I get really fired up, especially about my craft business, and then it kind of takes over everything.\n\nAt first it feels good, like I’m finally on track and I can handle all of it, the business and the house and everybody just needs to follow the plan. I’ll start making rules about how things should be done at home so I can focus, and in my head it feels completely reasonable. But then if my husband says even one small thing—like questioning a plan or saying maybe I’m doing too much—I’ll snap at him. I really feel in that moment like he’s trying to hold me back.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I can confidently count it as at least four times a year, but it’s happened enough that it’s a pattern, not just a one-time thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel depressed after these periods of high energy are over?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. That’s actually one of the parts that scares me a little, because when the energy is there, I don’t really see it as a problem in the moment. I’m just moving, planning, pushing things forward, arguing if I feel like people are slowing me down. But after it’s over, it’s like everything drops out from under me.\n\nI’ll be sitting at the kitchen table, and it’s just this heavy, empty feeling. Like I used everything up and there’s nothing left in me. And I’ll cry—sometimes I don’t even know what I’m crying about. It’s not always one specific thing. It’s more like I can’t get myself back to normal, and I feel stuck there, ashamed and exhausted and sad.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your periods of feeling depressed happen just as often as your periods of high energy?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, or at least they come pretty tied together for me. It’s hard for me to count them like one-for-one, because when I’m in that high-energy part, I’m not thinking, “Oh, this is an episode” or anything. I’m just going, going, going—running around, getting things done, arguing, pushing whatever plan I’ve got in my head.\n\nBut then afterward, almost like my body and mind just give out, I drop into that heavy, empty feeling. I’ll sit at the kitchen table and just feel like there’s nothing left in me. And I cry over nothing sometimes, or over things that don’t really explain how bad I feel. It’s like I can’t get myself back. So I wouldn’t say I’ve tracked it perfectly, but the depressed part does seem to follow those high-energy stretches pretty often.\n\nDoctor: How long do these periods of high energy typically last?\n\nPatient: It’s been about three weeks this time, I think. Not all exactly the same every single day, but the whole stretch of it — being wound up and restless, like I can’t settle myself — and then it getting stranger, like hearing my name sometimes, and then afterward just crashing… that’s been going on for around three weeks.\n\nI don’t know if I’m explaining it right, because it’s not like I wake up and mark a start date, but looking back, it feels like about three weeks where I haven’t really felt like myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you return to a normal state between these periods of high energy?\n\nPatient: Not really… or if I do, it doesn’t feel like it lasts long enough for me to trust it. After all that running around, pushing, arguing with people, feeling like I have to keep doing things, I don’t just land back in the middle. I kind of fall past normal, if that makes sense.\n\nI end up at the kitchen table a lot, just sitting there, and it’s like this heavy, empty feeling comes over me. I’ll cry, and sometimes there isn’t even a real reason I can point to. It’s just like everything in me got used up, and I can’t get myself back to how I’m supposed to be. So I guess between them, I’m not really “normal” so much as worn out and sad and trying to pull myself back together.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these periods of high energy are separate episodes, or do they kind of blend together?\n\nPatient: They kind of blend together, I think. It’s hard for me to put clean lines around it, like “this started here and ended there,” because it feels more like one whole stretch where I changed and then kept changing.\n\nThis time, from when I first started feeling really wound up and restless, and then it got to where I was hearing my name, and then I kind of crashed after that… that whole thing has been going on about three weeks. So I don’t know if I’d call them separate episodes exactly. Looking back, it feels like one long period where I haven’t been myself, with different parts to it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It’s hard for me to put clean lines around it, like “this started here and ended there,” because it feels more like one whole stretch where I changed and then kept changing.\n\nThis time, from when I first started feeling really wound up and restless, and then it got to where I was hearing my name, and then I kind of crashed after that… that whole thing has been going on about three weeks. So I don’t know if I’d call them separate episodes exactly. Looking back, it feels like one long period where I haven’t been myself, with different parts to it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're back to your normal self between these periods of high energy, or is it more like you're always somewhere on this spectrum of feeling energized or feeling down?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s more like I’m always somewhere on that swing, not really back to myself in the middle. After the high-energy part — all the running around, getting worked up, arguing with people, feeling like I’m pushing and pushing — I don’t just calm down and feel normal.

I kind of drop. Like everything gets heavy and empty all at once. I’ll end up sitting at the kitchen table, just sitting there, and I’ll start crying over nothing. Not even because something happened right then, just… crying because I can’t seem to get myself back. It feels like I used up whatever I had in me, and then there’s just this hollow feeling left.

So no, I wouldn’t say I’m really “normal” between them. It’s more like I go from too much to not enough, if that makes sense.
